### 🤖 Claude · 13:00:42

I read the current setup. Here's the important reality before we design this, then two decisions I need from you.

## Where things stand today

- **The "phone app" is a web page**, not a native app — the phone loads the Deck (`src/components/Deck.tsx`) in its browser from the Next.js server.
- **Auth is a static `DECK_TOKEN`** typed in by hand, sent as a plaintext header over **plain HTTP**.
- **The "host" is a Node CLI script** (`host-agent/agent.mjs`), not an installed app.

The security gap you're pointing at is real: over plain HTTP on shared WiFi, anyone sniffing (or ARP-spoofing to sit in the middle) can read that token and **inject or alter actions** — and remember the deck can run arbitrary `shell`, so that's a full remote-code path. A static token doesn't fix tampering; it just adds one reusable secret to steal.

## The design that actually meets "no one on the WiFi can tamper"

Tamper-resistance on a LAN needs **encryption + authentication of both ends**, bootstrapped out-of-band. The QR code is that out-of-band channel:

1. **Desktop app generates a fresh 32-byte key per pairing** and shows a QR encoding `http://<lan-ip>:<port>/pair#k=<key>&id=<pairId>`.
2. **You scan it with the phone's native camera**, which opens the deck URL. The key rides in the URL **fragment** (`#k=…`) — fragments are never sent over the network, so the key never crosses the wire. The app reads it, stores it, and strips it from history.
3. **Every action message is AES-256-GCM encrypted + signed** with that shared key and a monotonic counter. The desktop rejects anything with a bad tag or a stale counter.

Net effect: an attacker on the same WiFi sees only ciphertext — they **can't read, forge, replay, or tamper** with a single bit without the auth tag failing. That's exactly your requirement, and it also gets you confidentiality for free.

**The one gotcha that shapes everything:** browsers block the camera *and* Web Crypto over `http://192.168.x.x` (non-secure context). The "native camera opens the URL with the key in the fragment" trick sidesteps the camera limit, and we do the crypto with a tiny audited pure-JS lib (`@noble/ciphers`) instead of `crypto.subtle`. This is why I'd **keep the browser client** rather than fight LAN TLS certificate warnings.

Two forks are genuinely yours to decide:

### 🤖 Claude · 13:05:13

The native + Electron choice actually **simplifies and hardens** the design: with a native phone client we no longer serve a web page to the phone, so the Electron app becomes the *direct* TLS endpoint the phone talks to, and we can pin a real certificate (native TLS clients pin cleanly — no browser warnings). The Next.js SSE relay drops out of the critical path.

Let me lock the design into a plan doc and start building the host's testable core (headless, so I can verify it before we add the Electron window and the phone app).

````
# Secure pairing — Electron host + native phone app

Turns streamPhoneDeck into: an **installable desktop app** (Electron, macOS/Windows)
that the phone pairs with by **scanning a QR code**, over a channel that no other
device or person on the same Wi‑Fi can read, forge, replay, or tamper with.

## Why the architecture changes

Today the "phone app" is a **web page** served by Next.js, authed by a static
`DECK_TOKEN` sent in plaintext over HTTP. On shared Wi‑Fi that token can be
sniffed and actions injected — and the deck can run arbitrary `shell`, so that is
a full remote‑code path.

Going **native on the phone** removes the web page entirely and lets us pin a TLS
certificate on the client (native TLS clients pin cleanly; browsers can't without
scary warnings). So the **Electron desktop app becomes the TLS server the phone
talks to directly**. The Next.js SSE relay + separate Node agent collapse into one
process.

```
BEFORE:  phone browser ──HTTP token──▶ Next server ──SSE──▶ node agent ──▶ OS
AFTER:   native phone  ──pinned TLS + device token──▶ Electron host ──▶ OS
```

## The pairing / security protocol

Threat model: an attacker on the same Wi‑Fi (passive sniffing, or active
ARP‑spoof MITM) must not be able to read, forge, replay, tamper with, or enrol.

1. **First launch** — host generates a self‑signed cert+key (stored in userData),
   and computes its **SPKI SHA‑256 fingerprint** (survives cert renewal if the key
   is reused).
2. **Pair** — user clicks *Pair a phone
… [truncated, 3067 more chars]
````

```
// Self-signed cert lifecycle + SPKI pin.
//
// The host is its own certificate authority-of-one: it makes a self-signed cert
// on first run and keeps it. The phone doesn't trust it via any CA — it pins the
// SPKI (public-key) SHA-256 fingerprint we hand it in the pairing QR. Pinning the
// SPKI rather than the whole cert means a future cert renewal that reuses the key
// won't break existing pairings.

import { existsSync, mkdirSync, readFileSync, writeFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { join } from "node:path";
import crypto from "node:crypto";
import selfsigned from "selfsigned";

/** base64(SHA-256(DER SubjectPublicKeyInfo)) for a PEM cert. */
export function spkiFingerprint(certPem) {
  const spki = new crypto.X509Certificate(certPem).publicKey.export({
    type: "spki",
    format: "der",
  });
  return crypto.createHash("sha256").update(spki).digest("base64");
}

/** Same pin, computed from a peer's raw DER cert (what a TLS client sees). */
export function spkiFingerprintFromDer(der) {
  const spki = new crypto.X509Certificate(der).publicKey.export({
    type: "spki",
    format: "der",
  });
  return crypto.createHash("sha256").update(spki).digest("base64");
}

  mkdirSync(dir, { recursive: true });
  const certPath = join(dir, "cert.pem");
  const keyPath = join(dir, "key.pem");

    key = readFileSync(keyPath, "ut
```

```
// OS action executor — ported from host-agent/agent.mjs.
//
// Kept as a dependency-free ESM module so it can run inside the Electron main
// process (later) and be imported by tests. Dispatches by action type:
//   app   -> open -a / start / bare exec
//   url   -> open / start / xdg-open
//   keys  -> AppleScript System Events (macOS only)
//   shell -> raw exec (arbitrary — gated behind pairing at the network layer)

import { exec } from "node:child_process";
import { promisify } from "node:util";

const execAsync = promisify(exec);
const platform = process.platform; // 'darwin' | 'win32' | 'linux'

const VALID_TYPES = new Set(["app", "url", "keys", "shell"]);

/** Minimal shape check mirroring the Next app's validateAction intent. */
export function validateAction(action) {
  if (!action || typeof action !== "object") return null;
  const { type } = action;
  if (!VALID_TYPES.has(type)) return null;
  const field = type === "app" || type === "url" ? "target" : type === "keys" ? "combo" : "command";
  const value = action[field];
  if (typeof value !== "string" || value.length === 0 || value.length > 4096) return null;
  return { type, [field]: value };
}

  if (dryRun) {

    return;
```

```
  return "127.0.0.1";
```

```
 * verification (the cert is self-signed) and instead REQUIRE that the peer's
 * public-key hash equals the one from the QR — exactly what the phone will do.
 */
function pinnedRequest(path, { method = "GET", body, token, pin = info.fingerprint } = {}) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {

        port: info.port,
        path,
        method,
        rejectUnauthorized: false,
        checkServerIdentity: (_host, cert) => {
          const seen = spkiFingerprintFromDer(cert.raw);
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:07:40

Good catch from the test — that's exactly why we verify. The failure is real: with `rejectUnauthorized: false` (required for a self‑signed cert), Node **ignores** `checkServerIdentity` errors, so the pin wasn't actually enforced. The correct pattern is to inspect the peer cert on `secureConnect` and destroy the socket on mismatch. Let me fix the client.

**🔧 Edit**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/desktop/src/test-client.mjs`
```diff
- /**
 * TLS request that pins the server's SPKI fingerprint. We turn OFF CA
 * verification (the cert is self-signed) and instead REQUIRE that the peer's
 * public-key hash equals the one from the QR — exactly what the phone will do.
 */
function pinnedRequest(path, { method = "GET", body, token, pin = info.fingerprint } = {}) {
  return new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
    const req = https.request(
      {
        host: "127.0.0.1", // loopback for the test; phone would use info.host
        port: info.port,
        path,
        method,
        rejectUnauthorized: false,
        checkServerIdentity: (_host, cert) => {
          const seen = spkiFingerprintFromDer(cert.raw);
          if (seen !== pin) return new Error(`pin mismatch: ${seen} != ${pin}`);
          return undefined; // pin OK
        },
        headers: {
          "content-type": "application/json",
          ...(token ? { authorization: `Bearer ${token}` } : {}),
        },
      },
      (res) => {
        let raw = "";
        res.on("data", (c) => (raw += c));
        res.on("end", () => resolve({ status: res.statusCode, body: raw ? JSON.parse(raw) : null }));
      },
    );
    req.on("error", reject);
    if (body) req.write(JSON.stringify(body));
    req.end();
  });
}
+ /**
 * TLS request that pins the server's SPKI fingerprint. The cert is self-signed,
 * so CA verification is off (`rejectUnauthorized: false`). Node then *ignores*
 * `checkServerIdentity` failures, so we can't pin there — inste
… [truncated, 1339 more chars]
```

### 🤖 Claude · 13:08:45

**Stage 1a is built and verified.** The security core of the design is working headlessly, before any GUI or phone code exists.

## What landed

A new self-contained `desktop/` host package (plain Node ESM, so it's testable without a display — the Electron window wraps it later):

| File | Role |
|---|---|
| `desktop/src/cert.mjs` | Generates/loads the self-signed cert, computes the **SPKI SHA-256 pin** |
| `desktop/src/executor.mjs` | Ported OS executor (app/url/keys/shell) + a JS `validateAction` |
| `desktop/src/host.mjs` | The **HTTPS server**: `/health`, `/pair`, `/action` + single-use pairing codes and a device-token allowlist. Run directly, it prints the pairing QR to the terminal |
| `desktop/src/test-client.mjs` | Headless stand-in for the phone: does the real **cert-pinned** pairing flow + the negative tests |
| `SECURE-PAIRING-PLAN.md` | Architecture, the exact protocol, and the staged plan |

## Verification (all passing)

```
1. health over pinned TLS                 ✓ server healthy behind the pin
2. MITM check — wrong pin rejected        ✓ blocks impersonation
3. action without a token                 ✓ 401 unauthenticated
4. redeem pairing code → device token     ✓ enrolment works
5. reuse the single-use code              ✓ 403, single-use enforced
6. authenticated action                   ✓ executed (dry-run)
```

Check #2 initially **failed and caught a real bug**: with a self-signed cert you must set `rejectUnauthorized: false`, and Node then silently ignores `checkServerIdentity`, so my first pin wasn't enforced at all. Fixed by verifying the peer's SPKI on `secureConnect` and destroying the socket on mismatch — the effective equivalent of native pinning. Without that test, the "secure" channel would have accepted any MITM cert.

You can see it yourself locally:
```
cd desktop && npm run host      # prints the pairing QR + SPKI pin in the terminal
```

## What this already guarantees on shared Wi-Fi

TLS (confidentiality + integrity) · SPKI pin (no MITM, even with a self-signed cert) · per-device token (individually revocable) · single-use expiring code sent only inside pinned TLS (only someone looking at the QR can enrol). An attacker on the same network can't read, forge, replay, or enrol.

## Next stages (from the plan)

- **1b — Electron shell**: tray icon + a *Pair a phone* window that renders the QR, plus device list/revoke. This is the "installable app" wrapper.
- **2 — Expo phone app**: scan → pin → redeem → store token → fire actions. First real end-to-end on a phone.

Want me to continue with **1b (the Electron app)** next, or jump to **2 (the Expo phone app)**? I'd suggest 1b first so the QR has a real window to live in — but the phone app is the more satisfying thing to see working.

```diff
    const payload = { v: PROTOCOL_VERSION, name, host: lanIPv4(), port, fingerprint, code };
    // pairing.json is for the headless test-client / dev; the Electron shell
    // renders the QR in a window instead of reading this file. expiresAt is
    // returned for the UI countdown but kept out of the QR payload.
    writeFileSync(join(dir, "pairing.json"), JSON.stringify(payload, null, 2));
    return { payload, expiresAt };
  }
```

```
// Preload bridge for the pairing window. CommonJS (.cjs) so it loads cleanly in a
// sandboxed renderer. Exposes a tiny, explicit API — no Node internals reach the
// page. The renderer never touches the token store or the cert; it only asks the
// main process to start a pairing session and manage devices.

const { contextBridge, ipcRenderer } = require("electron");

contextBridge.exposeInMainWorld("deck", {
  startPairing: () => ipcRenderer.invoke("pairing:start"),
  listDevices: () => ipcRenderer.invoke("devices:list"),
  revoke: (deviceId) => ipcRenderer.invoke("devices:revoke", deviceId),
  onDevicesChanged: (cb) => {
    const handler = () => cb();
    ipcRenderer.on("devices:changed", handler);
    return () => ipcRenderer.removeListener("devices:changed", handler);
  },
});
```

```
const CRC_TABLE = (() => {
  const t = new Uint32Array(256);
  for (let n = 0; n < 256; n++) {
    let c = n;
    for (let k = 0; k < 8; k++) c = c & 1 ? 0xedb88320 ^ (c >>> 1) : c >>> 1;
    t[n] = c >>> 0;
  }
  return t;
})();

function crc32(buf) {
  let c = 0xffffffff;
  for (let i = 0; i < buf.length; i++) c = CRC_TABLE[(c ^ buf[i]) & 0xff] ^ (c >>> 8);
  return (c ^ 0xffffffff) >>> 0;
}

function chunk(type, data) {
  const len = Buffer.alloc(4);

  const body = Buffer.concat([Buffer.from(type, "ascii"), data]);
  const crc = Buffer.alloc(4);
  crc.writeUInt32BE(crc32(body), 0);
  return Buffer.concat([len, body, crc]);
}
```

**Stage 1b is built and boot-verified.** Electron 34 loaded the ESM main process, started the host core inside it (listening with a fresh SPKI pin), and exited cleanly — no stray windows on your screen.

## What landed (`desktop/`)

| File | Role |
|---|---|
| `main.mjs` | Electron main: starts the host core, lives in the **menu bar (tray)**, opens the pairing window, wires IPC |
| `preload.cjs` | Locked-down bridge (`window.deck.startPairing / listDevices / revoke`) — no Node internals reach the page |
| `pair.html` | The **pairing window**: live QR, address, single-use code with a 2-min countdown, SPKI pin (copyable), and a paired-devices list with Unpair |
| `scripts/gen-icon.mjs` + `assets/*` | Generates the black+alpha menu-bar template icon (a little button-grid tile) |

`package.json` now has `start` (GUI), `smoke` (headless boot check), plus the earlier host/test scripts.

## See it yourself

I deliberately didn't pop a window onto your screen. To view the tray app + QR window:

```
cd desktop && npm start          # menu-bar icon + pairing window
DECK_DRY_RUN=1 npm start         # same, but logs actions instead of running them
```

That's the whole desktop side: an installable-shaped app that shows a QR and manages paired phones. (Packaging into a `.dmg`/`.exe` installer is Stage 4.)

## Next: Stage 2 — the Expo phone app

One real constraint I need to flag before scaffolding, because it shapes everything: **true cert-pinning (and even talking to a self-signed HTTPS host) doesn't work in Expo Go.** RN's networking rejects self-signed certs, and native SSL pinning needs a config plugin. So the phone app must run as an **Expo *dev build*** (custom client), not Expo Go. That's fine — it's the correct path for the security model — but it means testing happens on a real device via a dev build, which you'll drive.

Two setup choices change what I scaffold and the run instructions:

### 🤖 Claude · 13:27:16

Great — both platforms, and CI-first. Let me answer the CI question directly, because it surfaced a **real design correction** I need to make before scaffolding the phone app.

## GitHub Actions vs EAS

- **Android: yes, fully in GitHub Actions.** An `ubuntu` runner with JDK + Android SDK does `expo prebuild` → `./gradlew assembleDebug`, producing an installable dev-client **APK artifact** you download and sideload. No paid account. This is the fast path.
- **iOS: technically yes, practically painful.** You need a `macos` runner **plus Apple code-signing** (a $99/yr Apple Developer account, certs, provisioning profiles) to install on a real iPhone. The *simulator* build needs no signing but only runs on a Mac. **EAS exists precisely to manage that signing** — and you can invoke `eas build` *from* a GitHub Actions workflow. So: **GH Actions for Android, EAS for iOS** (orchestrated from the same workflow) is the pragmatic combo. I'll set up both.

## The design correction (important)

While planning the RN side I hit a hard constraint: **our TLS cert-pinning approach doesn't fit React Native.** RN's `fetch` won't talk to a self-signed HTTPS host at all, and every pinning library (or Android's `network-security-config`) pins certs **known at build time** — but our pin is *per-user, learned from the QR at runtime*. Fighting that means a native TLS socket module and hand-rolled HTTP.

The clean fix — and it's actually **simpler and equally secure** — is to move the crypto **up a layer**: plain HTTP transport, but every message is **AES-256-GCM encrypted + authenticated with a per-device key delivered in the QR**, with a monotonic counter for replay protection. No TLS, no cert pinning, no native module — just `fetch` + a Hermes-compatible JS cipher on the phone, and Node's built-in crypto on the host.

This still fully meets your requirement — an on-path attacker on the Wi-Fi **can't read (confidentiality), can't forge or modify (GCM tag), can't replay (counter), can't inject (no key)**. Tampering is detected and rejected. I'll note ECDH forward-secrecy as a future hardening.

Let me rework the host to this protocol and re-verify it headlessly. Rewriting `host.mjs`:

**🔧 Write**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/desktop/src/host.mjs`

```
// streamPhoneDeck host core — the server the phone pairs with and talks to.
//
// Transport is plain HTTP; ALL security is application-layer. Each phone gets a
// random 32-byte key at pairing time (delivered inside the QR, so it never
// crosses the network). Every request/response is an AES-256-GCM envelope keyed
// by that device key, with a monotonic counter to stop replays. An attacker on
// the same Wi-Fi therefore can't read, forge, modify, replay, or inject — the
// GCM tag fails on any tamper and the counter rejects any resend.
//
// Headless on purpose: the Electron shell imports createHost() and drives
// startPairing() from a window. Run directly (`npm run host`) to print a QR.

const PROTOCOL_VERSION = 1;

    for (const a of addrs ?? []) {
      if (a.family === "IPv4" && !a.internal) return a.address;
    }
  }
  return "127.0.0.1";
}

// --- AES-256-GCM envelope (the phone mirrors these two functions) ------------

/** Encrypt an object -> { n, ct } (base64 nonce, base64 ciphertext‖tag). */
function seal(keyBuf, obj) {
  const
… [truncated, 6665 more chars]
```

```
// Envelope helpers — identical to the host's (the phone reimplements these too).
function seal(keyBuf, obj) {
  const iv = crypto.randomBytes(12);
  const c = crypto.createCipheriv("aes-256-gcm", keyBuf, iv);
  const ct = Buffer.concat([c.update(Buffer.from(JSON.stringify(obj))), c.final()]);
  return { n: iv.toString("base64"), ct: Buffer.concat([ct, c.getAuthTag()]).toString("base64") };
}

  d.setAuthTag(buf.subarray(buf.length - 16));
  return JSON.parse(Buffer.concat([d.update(buf.subarray(0, buf.length - 16)), d.final()]).toString());
}
```

```diff
   **device token** (32 random bytes), stores it in an allowlist, returns it. The
   phone keeps the token in secure storage (`expo-secure-store`).
5. **Use** — every `POST /action` carries the device token (Bearer) over the
   pinned TLS channel; host checks it (`timingSafeEqual`) then executes.

Why it holds:
- **TLS** → confidentiality + integrity (bit‑flips break the record MAC).
- **SPKI pin** → server authentication; blocks MITM even with a self‑signed cert.
- **Device token** → client authentication; each phone individually revocable.
- **Single‑use, expiring pairing code, only ever sent inside pinned TLS** → only a
  person physically looking at the QR can enrol; the code is never on the wire in
  the clear.
```

**🔧 Bash**

````
# Expo Camera QR Scanning API (SDK 57)

## Component & Permission Hook

- **Component**: `CameraView` from `'expo-camera'`
- **Permission Hook**: `useCameraPermissions()` - "Check or request permissions to access the camera"

## QR Scanning Setup

Enable QR scanning via the `barcodeScannerSettings` prop:

```tsx
<CameraView
  barcodeScannerSettings={{
    barcodeTypes: ["qr"],
  }}
  onBarcodeScanned={handleBarcode}
/>
```

## Callback Data Shape

The `onBarcodeScanned` callback receives a `BarcodeScanningResult` object with:

| Property | Type | Purpose |
|----------|------|---------|
| `type` | `string` | "The barcode type that was scanned" |
| `data` | `string` | "The information encoded in the barcode" |
| `bounds` | `BarcodeBounds` | Bounding box coordinates |
| `cornerPoints` | `BarcodePoint[]` | Corner points of detection area |

## Minimal Example

```tsx
import { CameraView, useCameraPermissions } from 'expo-camera';
import { useState } from 'react';
import { View, Text } from 'react-native';

export default function QRScanner() {
  const [permission, requestPermission] = useCameraPermissions();
  const [scannedData, setScannedData] = useState(null);

  if (!permission?.granted) {
    return <Text onPress={requestPermission}>Grant camera access</Text>;
  }

  return (
    <View style={{ flex: 1 }}>
      <CameraView
        style={{ flex: 1 }}
        barcodeScannerSettings={{ barcodeTypes: ["qr"] }}
        onBarcodeScanned={({ data }) => setScannedData(data)}
     
… [truncated, 76 more chars]
````

```
// AES-256-GCM envelopes — the phone side of the host's src/host.mjs seal/unseal.
// Wire format is identical: { n: base64(nonce), ct: base64(ciphertext‖tag) }.
// noble's gcm().encrypt appends the 16-byte tag, matching Node's Buffer.concat.

import "react-native-get-random-values"; // patches global crypto.getRandomValues
import { gcm } from "@noble/ciphers/aes.js";
import { utf8ToBytes, bytesToUtf8 } from "@noble/ciphers/utils.js";

const B64 = "ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZabcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz0123456789+/";

export function bytesToB64(bytes: Uint8Array): string {
  let out = "";
  for (let i = 0; i < bytes.length; i += 3) {
    const b0 = bytes[i];
    const b1 = bytes[i + 1];
    const b2 = bytes[i + 2];
    out += B64[b0 >> 2];
    out += B64[((b0 & 3) << 4) | ((b1 ?? 0) >> 4)];
    out += i + 1 < bytes.length ? B64[((b1 & 15) << 2) | ((b2 ?? 0) >> 6)] : "=";
    out += i + 2 < bytes.length ? B64[b2 & 63] : "=";
  }
  return out;
}

export function b64ToBytes(s: string): Uint8Array {
  const clean = s.replace(/[^A-Za-z0-9+/]/g, "");
  const out: number[] = [];
  let acc = 0;
  let bits = 0;
  for (let i = 0; i < clean.length; i++) {
    acc = (acc << 6) | B64.indexOf(clean[i]);
    bits += 6;
    if (bits >= 8) {
      bits -= 8;
      out.push((acc >> bits) & 0xff);
    }
  }
  return Uint8Array.from(out);
}

function randomBytes(n: number): Uint8Array {
  const b = new Uint8Array(n);
  (globalThis.crypto as Crypto).getRandomValues(b);
  return b;
}

export type Enve
```

```
// Persistent pairing state, kept in the OS secure keystore (Keychain / Keystore).
// The device key never touches AsyncStorage or plain files. The send counter
// lives here too so it keeps increasing across app restarts (replay protection).

import * as SecureStore from "expo-secure-store";

export type Pairing = {
  kid: string;
  key: string; // base64, 32 bytes
  host: string;
  port: number;
  name: string;
};

const PAIR_KEY = "spd.pairing";
const CTR_KEY = "spd.ctr";

export async function loadPairing(): Promise<Pairing | null> {
  const raw = await SecureStore.getItemAsync(PAIR_KEY);
  return raw ? (JSON.parse(raw) as Pairing) : null;
}

export async function savePairing(p: Pairing): Promise<void> {
  await SecureStore.setItemAsync(PAIR_KEY, JSON.stringify(p));
  await SecureStore.setItemAsync(CTR_KEY, "0");
}

export async function clearPairing(): Promise<void> {
  await SecureStore.deleteItemAsync(PAIR_KEY);
  await SecureStore.deleteItemAsync(CTR_KEY);
}

/** Atomic-enough monotonic counter for GCM message ordering. */
export async function nextCounter(): Promise<number> {
  const cur = parseInt((await SecureStore.getItemAsync(CTR_KEY)) ?? "0", 10) || 0;
  const next = cur + 1;
  await SecureStore.setItemAsync(CTR_KEY, String(next));
  return next;
}
```

`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/mobile/src/rpc.ts`
```
// Talks to the host's POST /rpc: seal a command, send it, unseal the reply.
// Sends are serialized by the caller (buttons disable while in flight) so the
// monotonic counter stays strictly increasing.

import { seal, unseal, b64ToBytes } from "./crypto";
import { nextCounter, type Pairing } from "./storage";

export type DeckAction =
  | { type: "app"; target: string }
  | { type: "url"; target: string }
  | { type: "keys"; combo: string }
  | { type: "shell"; command: string };

async function sendCmd<T = unknown>(p: Pairing, cmd: Record<string, unknown>): Promise<T> {
  const key = b64ToBytes(p.key);
  const ctr = await nextCounter();
  const envelope = { kid: p.kid, ...seal(key, { ctr, ...cmd }) };

  const res = await fetch(`http://${p.host}:${p.port}/rpc`, {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "content-type": "application/json" },
    body: JSON.stringify(envelope),
  });

  if (res.status !== 200) {
    const err = await res.json().catch(() => ({ error: `HTTP ${res.status}` }));
    throw new Error(err.error ?? `HTTP ${res.status}`);
  }
  const body = await res.json();
  return unseal<T>(key, body.n, body.ct);
}

export function hello(p: Pairing, name: string) {
  return sendCmd<{ ok: boolean; host: string }>(p, { cmd: "hello", name });
}

export function sendAction(p: Pairing, label: string, action: DeckAction) {
  return sendCmd<{ ok: boolean; error?: string }>(p, { cmd: "action", label, action });
}
```

```
// Proves the phone's noble AES-256-GCM and the host's Node crypto are wire-
// compatible in BOTH directions — the one thing that can silently break the
// pairing and that we can check without a device. Run: node scripts/interop-check.mjs

import { gcm } from "@noble/ciphers/aes.js";
import crypto from "node:crypto";

const key = Uint8Array.from(crypto.randomBytes(32));
const enc = new TextEncoder();
const dec = new TextDecoder();

// Phone side (noble), mirroring mobile/src/crypto.ts
function phoneSeal(obj) {
  const nonce = Uint8Array.from(crypto.randomBytes(12));
  const ct = gcm(key, nonce).encrypt(enc.encode(JSON.stringify(obj)));
  return { n: Buffer.from(nonce).toString("base64"), ct: Buffer.from(ct).toString("base64") };
}
function phoneUnseal(n, ctB64) {
  const pt = gcm(key, Uint8Array.from(Buffer.from(n, "base64"))).decrypt(Uint8Array.from(Buffer.from(ctB64, "base64")));
  return JSON.parse(dec.decode(pt));
}

// Host side (Node crypto), mirroring desktop/src/host.mjs
function hostSeal(obj) {
  const iv = crypto.randomBytes(12);
  const c = crypto.createCipheriv("aes-256-gcm", key, iv);
  const ct = Buffer.concat([c.update(Buffer.from(JSON.stringify(obj))), c.final()]);
  return { n: iv.toString("base64"), ct: Buffer.concat([ct, c.getAuthTag()]).toString("base64") };
}
function hostUnseal(n, ctB64) {
  const iv = Buffer.from(n, "base64");
  const buf = Buffer.from(ctB64, "base64");
  const d = crypto.createDecipheriv("aes-256-gcm", key, iv);
  d.setAuthTag(buf.su
```

```
name: mobile-android-dev-build

# Builds an installable Android dev-client APK entirely inside GitHub Actions —
# no EAS, no paid account. Download the APK from the run's Artifacts and sideload
# it onto a phone (adb install / open the file). Then `npx expo start --dev-client`
# on your machine and the app connects to it.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths:
      - "mobile/**"
      - ".github/workflows/mobile-android.yml"

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: mobile
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 20
          cache: npm
          cache-dependency-path: mobile/package-lock.json

      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: 17

      - name: Install dependencies
        run: npm ci

      - name: Prebuild native Android project
        run: npx expo prebuild --platform android --no-install

      - name: Build debug APK (includes the dev client)
        run: |
          chmod +x android/gradlew
          cd android && ./gradlew assembleDebug --no-daemon

      - uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: streamphonedeck-android-dev
          path: mobile/android/app/build/outputs/apk/debug/app-debug.apk
          if-no-files-found: error
```

```
name: mobile-ios-eas-build

# iOS dev builds need Apple code-signing, which EAS manages for you. This runs an
# EAS cloud build from CI. One-time setup before it works:
#   1. cd mobile && npx eas init          (creates the Expo project + projectId)
#   2. Add an EXPO_TOKEN repo secret       (Expo account → Access tokens)
#   3. For a REAL iPhone: profile "development" (needs an Apple Developer account,
#      EAS provisions certs). For the SIMULATOR: profile "development-simulator"
#      (no Apple account needed) — change the --profile below.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      profile:
        description: "EAS build profile"
        default: "development"
        type: choice
        options:
          - development
          - development-simulator

jobs:
  build:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: mobile
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 20
          cache: npm
          cache-dependency-path: mobile/package-lock.json

      - name: Install dependencies
        run: npm ci

      - name: Install EAS CLI
        run: npm i -g eas-cli

      - name: EAS build (iOS)
        env:
          EXPO_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.EXPO_TOKEN }}
        run: eas build --platform ios --profile "${{ inputs.profile || 'development' }}" --non-interactive --no-wait
```

````
# streamPhoneDeck — phone app (Expo)

Native iOS/Android client. Scans the desktop host's pairing QR, then sends
button presses over an **AES-256-GCM encrypted channel** (see
[`../SECURE-PAIRING-PLAN.md`](../SECURE-PAIRING-PLAN.md)).

> **Needs a dev build — not Expo Go.** Expo Go can't reach a self-hosted device
> on your LAN (iOS Local Network permission + ATS, Android cleartext all require
> the native config baked into this project). Build a dev client, then run Metro.

## Structure

- `src/crypto.ts` — AES-256-GCM `seal`/`unseal`, wire-identical to the host
  (`desktop/src/host.mjs`). Verified by `scripts/interop-check.mjs`.
- `src/storage.ts` — pairing (device key) + send counter in the OS secure store.
- `src/rpc.ts` — seals a command → `POST /rpc` → unseals the reply.
- `App.tsx` — pair screen (QR scanner) → deck screen (starter tiles).

## Run it

**Android (fastest — no Apple account):**
```bash
# In CI: the "mobile-android-dev-build" workflow produces an APK artifact.
# Locally instead:
cd mobile
npx expo run:android          # builds + installs the dev client on a device/emulator
# then it starts Metro; or later: npx expo start --dev-client
```

**iOS:**
```bash
cd mobile
npx expo run:ios              # local, needs Xcode
# or a cloud build (managed signing):
npx eas init                  # one time — creates the Expo project
eas build -p ios --profile development           # real device
eas build -p ios --profile development-simulator # simulator, no Apple accoun
… [truncated, 830 more chars]
````

## Where we are: Stages 1–2 done and verified

Three parts now exist, with everything that *can* be checked without a phone actually checked:

**`desktop/` — the installable host (Electron menu-bar app)**
- Plain-HTTP transport, **all security app-layer**: per-device AES-256-GCM key delivered in the QR, monotonic counter for replay.
- Tray icon + *Pair a phone* window (live QR, pairing status, device list/unpair).
- ✅ Verified: `node src/test-client.mjs` passes all 6 checks — **forgery, tamper, and replay are rejected**; Electron boots the core (`npm run smoke`).

**`mobile/` — the phone app (Expo SDK 57, iOS + Android)**
- QR scan (`CameraView`) → device key in the OS secure store → starter tiles that seal actions over `/rpc`.
- Native config for LAN access baked in (iOS Local Network + ATS, Android cleartext) via config plugins.
- ✅ Verified: `tsc --noEmit` clean, and `scripts/interop-check.mjs` proves the phone's noble crypto and the host's Node crypto are **wire-compatible both ways** (the highest-risk seam).

**`.github/workflows/` — CI (as you asked)**
- **Android fully in GitHub Actions**: `expo prebuild` + `gradlew assembleDebug` → downloadable dev-client **APK**. No EAS, no paid account.
- **iOS via EAS** (managed Apple signing), dispatchable with device or simulator profile.

## Honest gaps

- **The phone app has not run on a device** — camera scan, secure-store, and RN rendering are unverified at runtime (only types + crypto interop are proven). That first real end-to-end is yours to drive via a dev build.
- **iOS CI needs one-time setup**: `cd mobile && npx eas init` + an `EXPO_TOKEN` repo secret. A *real iPhone* also needs an Apple Developer account (simulator profile doesn't).
- **`keys` is macOS-only** and needs Accessibility permission; **`shell` is arbitrary RCE by design**, now gated behind pairing.

## To see it right now

```
cd desktop && npm start          # tray app + QR window on your Mac
```
Then produce a phone build (Android is fastest): trigger the **mobile-android-dev-build** workflow, install the APK, `cd mobile && npx expo start --dev-client`, and scan the QR.

## What's left

- **Stage 3** — full deck UX on the phone: editable grid, icons, all four action types, persistence (parity with the old web deck).
- **Stage 4** — package the desktop app into a real `.dmg`/`.exe` installer (`electron-builder`), auto-reconnect, launch-at-login.

```
const MODIFIERS = new Set(["cmd", "command", "ctrl", "control", "alt", "opt", "option", "shift"]);

const SPECIAL_KEYS = new Set([
  "return", "enter", "tab", "space", "delete", "backspace", "escape", "esc",
  "left", "right", "down", "up", "home", "end", "pageup", "pagedown",

  shift: "⇧",
};

export type ParsedCombo = { ok: true; pretty: string } | { ok: false; error: string };

export function parseCombo(input: string): ParsedCombo {
  const combo = input.trim();
  if (!combo) return { ok: false, error: "Enter a shortcut" };

  const parts = combo.toLowerCase().split("+").map((p) => p.trim()).filter(Boolean);
  const mods: string[] = [];
  const keys: string[] = [];
  for (const part of parts) {
    if (MODIFIERS.has(part)) mods.push(part);
    else keys.push(part);
  }

  if (keys.length === 0) return { ok: false, error: "Missing a key (e.g. cmd+space)" };
  if (keys.length > 1) return { ok: false, error: "Only one non-modifier key allowed" };
```

```
// Per-device deck layout, persisted in AsyncStorage (the pairing key lives in the
// secure store instead — see storage.ts). defaultButtons seed a fresh device;
// reset clears back to them. Mirrors the web app's src/lib/storage.ts.

import AsyncStorage from "@react-native-async-storage/async-storage";
import { defaultButtons, newButtonId, validateAction, type DeckButton } from "./buttons";

const DECK_KEY = "streamphonedeck.deck.v1";

export async function loadDeck(): Promise<DeckButton[]> {
  try {
    const raw = await AsyncStorage.getItem(DECK_KEY);
    if (!raw) return defaultButtons;
    const parsed = JSON.parse(raw);
    if (!Array.isArray(parsed)) return defaultButtons;
    const clean = parsed.map(coerceButton).filter((b): b is DeckButton => b !== null);
    return clean.length ? clean : defaultButtons;
  } catch {
    return defaultButtons;
  }
}

export async function saveDeck(buttons: DeckButton[]): Promise<void> {
  await AsyncStorage.setItem(DECK_KEY, JSON.stringify(buttons));
}

export async function resetDeck(): Promise<DeckButton[]> {
  await AsyncStorage.removeItem(DECK_KEY);
  return defaultButtons;
}

function coerceButton(input: unknown): DeckButton | null {
  if (!input || typeof input !== "object") return null;
  const b = input as Record<string, unknown>;
  const action = validateAction(b.action);

  return {

    label: typeof b.label === "string" ? b.label :
```

```
            setPairing(null);
          }}
        />
      ) : (
        <PairScreen onPaired={setPairing} />
      )}
      <StatusBar style="light" />
    </View>
  );
}
```

## What landed in `mobile/`

- **`src/buttons.ts`** — ported data model: `ButtonAction` union, `defaultButtons`, `TILE_COLORS` (real hex), plus `makeAction`/`validateAction`/`describeAction`.
- **`src/keys.ts`** — `parseCombo` for live shortcut validation/preview (mirrors the host's key names).
- **`src/deckStorage.ts`** — layout persisted in AsyncStorage, seeded from defaults, with the same defensive `coerceButton` sanitizing as the web app.
- **`src/TileEditor.tsx`** — slide-up modal: label, emoji quick-picks, action-type chips, per-type value field (with `⌘ ⇧ 4`-style live preview for shortcuts), color swatches, delete.
- **`App.tsx`** — deck screen now has **Edit mode**: tap-to-edit, delete badges, an Add tile, and Reset to defaults. Outside edit mode, tiles fire actions.

## Status across the whole build

| Stage | State |
|---|---|
| 1a Host core (encrypted protocol) | ✅ verified (forge/tamper/replay rejected) |
| 1b Electron tray + QR window | ✅ boot-verified |
| 2 Phone: pair → connect + CI | ✅ types + crypto interop verified |
| 3 Editable deck on phone | ✅ typechecks |
| 4 Package `.dmg`/`.exe` installer | ⬜ not started |

## My recommendation before Stage 4

Three of four stages are **code-complete but never run on a real phone**. Packaging an installer around an app that hasn't done a single real end-to-end press feels backwards — I'd **validate on a device first**:

1. `cd desktop && npm start` → **Pair a phone** (QR window).
2. Trigger the **mobile-android-dev-build** workflow (Actions tab) → download the APK → install on an Android phone. *(Or `cd mobile && npx expo run:android` locally.)*
3. Open the app, scan the QR, press a tile — your Mac should react. Watch the `desktop` terminal for `✓ Paired` / `▶ …` lines.

If it misbehaves, that's exactly the kind of concrete failure I can chase down.

**So: want me to proceed to Stage 4 (package the desktop installer), or would you rather do the device run first and report back what happens?** Say "proceed" and I'll take Stage 4; tell me what you see from the device run and I'll debug it.

### 🤖 Claude · 11:34:04

Now extend the icon generator to also produce a colored 1024×1024 app icon (for the installer), adding an RGBA PNG path:

**🔧 Edit**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/desktop/scripts/gen-icon.mjs`
```diff
- mkdirSync(join(__dirname, "..", "assets"), { recursive: true });
for (const [name, size] of [["trayTemplate.png", 22], ["trayTemplate@2x.png", 44]]) {
  writeFileSync(join(__dirname, "..", "assets", name), encodePng(size, tileAlpha(size)));
}
console.log("wrote assets/trayTemplate.png, assets/trayTemplate@2x.png");
+ // 8-bit truecolor + alpha PNG (color type 6), for the colored app icon.
function encodePngRGBA(size, rgbaAt) {
  const raw = Buffer.alloc(size * (1 + size * 4));
  let o = 0;
  for (let y = 0; y < size; y++) {
    raw[o++] = 0; // filter: none
    for (let x = 0; x < size; x++) {
      const [r, g, b, a] = rgbaAt(x, y);
      raw[o++] = r;
      raw[o++] = g;
      raw[o++] = b;
      raw[o++] = a;
    }
  }
  const ihdr = Buffer.alloc(13);
  ihdr.writeUInt32BE(size, 0);
  ihdr.writeUInt32BE(size, 4);
  ihdr[8] = 8; // bit depth
  ihdr[9] = 6; // color type: truecolor + alpha
  const sig = Buffer.from([137, 80, 78, 71, 13, 10, 26, 10]);
  return Buffer.concat([
    sig,
    chunk("IHDR", ihdr),
    chunk("IDAT", zlib.deflateSync(raw)),
    chunk("IEND", Buffer.alloc(0)),
  ]);
}

// App icon: a rounded-square blue gradient with a 2x2 grid of light tiles.
function appIcon(size) {
```

````
# streamPhoneDeck — desktop host

The installable app that runs on your computer, pairs with the phone via a QR
code, and executes actions (launch apps, open URLs, macOS shortcuts, shell). See
[`../SECURE-PAIRING-PLAN.md`](../SECURE-PAIRING-PLAN.md) for the protocol.

Transport is plain HTTP; **all security is application-layer** — each phone gets a
32-byte key from the QR and every message is an AES-256-GCM envelope with a
monotonic counter. An attacker on the same Wi-Fi can't read, forge, replay, or
inject.

## Structure

- `src/host.mjs` — the TLS-free HTTP host: `/health`, `/rpc`, per-device keys,
  envelope seal/unseal, replay counter. Runnable headless (`npm run host`).
- `src/executor.mjs` — OS action executor (app/url/keys/shell).
- `main.mjs` — Electron shell: menu-bar tray, pairing window, IPC.
- `preload.cjs` / `pair.html` — the pairing window bridge + UI.
- `src/test-client.mjs` — headless stand-in for the phone (security tests).
- `scripts/gen-icon.mjs` — generates the tray template + colored app icon.

## Develop

```bash
npm start                 # menu-bar app + pairing window
DECK_DRY_RUN=1 npm start  # log actions instead of running them
npm run smoke             # boot the core inside Electron and exit (no UI)

npm run host              # run just the host in a terminal (prints a QR)
npm run test:client       # prove forge/tamper/replay are rejected (host must run)
```

## Package installers

```bash
npm run pack       # unpacked .app/.exe dir in dist/ (fas
… [truncated, 722 more chars]
````

```
// Proves the phone's noble X25519 + HKDF + HMAC agree with the host's Node crypto,

import { x25519 } from "@noble/curves/ed25519.js";
import { hkdf } from "@noble/hashes/hkdf.js";

import { sha256 } from "@noble/hashes/sha2.js";

const hostRawPub = Buffer.from(hostKp.publicKey.export({ format: "jwk" }).x, "base64url");
const importPub = (raw32) =>
  crypto.createPublicKey({ key: { kty: "OKP", crv: "X25519", x: Buffer.from(raw32).toString("base64url") }, format: "jwk" });

// Phone (noble) keypair.
const phonePriv = crypto.randomBytes(32);
const phoneRawPub = x25519.getPublicKey(phonePriv);

console.log("1. shared secret matches across implementations");
const sharedNode = crypto.diffieHellman({ privateKey: hostKp.privateKey, publicKey: importPub(phoneRawPub) });
const sharedNoble = x25519.getSharedSecret(phonePriv, hostRawPub);
assert(eq(sharedNode, sharedNoble), "Node(host priv · phone pub) == noble(phone priv · host pub)");

console.log("2. HKDF derives the same 32-byte key");
```

```
// streamPhoneDeck host core — the server the phone pairs with and talks to.
//
// Transport is plain HTTP; ALL security is application-layer.
//
// PAIRING (spd2, forward secret): the QR carries the host's EPHEMERAL X25519
// public key — never a secret. The phone generates its own ephemeral key, does
// ECDH, and HKDF-derives the shared device key. It proves it actually saw the QR
// (knows the host public key) with an HMAC confirmation tag, which also stops an
// on-path attacker from hijacking the pairing. A screenshot of the QR alone can't
// derive the key, and a later key leak doesn't expose past pairings.
//
// MESSAGES: every request/response is an AES-256-GCM envelope keyed by the device
// key, with a monotonic counter. An attacker on the Wi-Fi can't read, forge,
// modify, replay, or inject — the GCM tag fails on tamper, the counter on resend.

import http from "node:http";
import crypto from "node:crypto";
import os from "node:os";
import { existsSync, mkdirSync, readFileSync, writeFileSync } from "node:fs";
import { join } from "node:path";
import { fileURLToPath } from "node:url";
import qrcode from "qrcode";
import { execute, validateAction } from "./executor.mjs";

const PROTOCOL = "spd2";
const PROTOCOL_VERSION = 2;
const HKDF_INFO = Buffer.from("spd-pair-v2", "utf8");

function lanIPv4() {
  for (const addrs of Object.values(os.networkInterfaces())) {
    for (const a of addrs ?? []) {
      if (a.family === "IPv4" && !a.internal) return a.address;
    }
  
… [truncated, 8743 more chars]
```

```
}
```

```diff
- export function unseal<T = unknown>(key: Uint8Array, n: string, ct: string): T {

+ export function unseal<T = unknown>(key: Uint8Array, n: string, ct: string): T {
  const pt = gcm(key, b64ToBytes(n)).decrypt(b64ToBytes(ct)); // throws on bad tag
  return JSON.parse(bytesToUtf8(pt)) as T;
}

// --- X25519 forward-secret pairing (mirrors desktop/src/host.mjs) ------------

const PAIR_INFO = utf8ToBytes("spd-pair-v2");

function concatBytes(...arrays: Uint8Array[]): Uint8Array {
  const out = new Uint8Array(arrays.reduce((n, a) => n + a.length, 0));
  let o = 0;
  for (const a of arrays) {
    out.set(a, o);
    o += a.length;
  }
  return out;
}

export function genEphemeral(): { priv: Uint8Array; pub: Uint8Array } {
  const priv = randomBytes(32);
  return { priv, pub: x25519.getPublicKey(priv) };
}

/** ECDH with the host's QR public key, then HKDF → the 32-byte device key. */
export function deriveDeviceKey(priv: Uint8Array, hostPub: Uint8Array, pid: string): Uint8Array {
  const shared = x25519.getSharedSecret(priv, hostPub);
  return hkdf(sha256, shared, utf8ToBytes(pid), PAIR_INFO, 32);
}

/** HMAC proving we saw the QR (knew the host public key). */
export function confirmTag(key: Uint8Array, pid: string, hostPub: Uint8Array, phonePub: Uint8Array): Uint8Array {
  return hmac(sha256, key, concatBytes(utf8ToBytes(pid), hostPub, phonePub));
}
```

```diff
- 1. **Pair** — user clicks *Pair a phone*. Host mints a fresh **32-byte device key**
   + `kid` (key id), stores a pending device (short TTL ~2 min), and renders a QR
   encoding JSON: `{ v, proto:"spd1", name, host:<lan-ip>, port, kid, k:<base64 key> }`.
2. **Scan** — the native app scans the QR and stores `{ kid, key, host, port }`.
   The key came out-of-band (the screen) and **never crosses the network**.
3. **Envelope protocol** — every request is `POST /rpc` with an **AES-256-GCM
   envelope** `{ kid, n, ct }` (nonce + ciphertext‖tag) sealed with the device key,
   whose plaintext carries a strictly-increasing `ctr` and a `cmd`. The host finds
   the device by `kid`, decrypts (tag verifies integrity + authenticity), rejects
   `ctr <= lastCtr` (replay), runs the command, and returns a sealed reply.
4. **Enrol / use** — first valid `cmd:"hello"` flips the device to *activated*
   (paired); `cmd:"action"` validates + executes. Revoke = delete the device key.

Why it holds (attacker on the same Wi-Fi, no QR):
- **AES-256-GCM** → confidentiality + integrity; any bit-flip fails the tag.
- **Per-device key, only ever shown on-screen** → client + server authentication;
  without it you can't read, forge, or inject. Each phone is individually revocable.
- **Monotonic counter** → replays rejected.
- Residual: an attacker can drop packets (DoS, unavoidable on a shared network)
  and see that encrypted blobs flow (traffic metadata), nothing more.

Future hardening: X25519 handsha
```

### 🤖 Claude · 11:57:02

**Forward-secrecy hardening (spd2) is done and verified.** Your original requirement — nobody on the Wi-Fi can get in or tamper — is now backed by a proper key-agreement, and the last real weakness (the QR containing the secret) is closed.

## What changed

The QR now carries an **ephemeral X25519 public key**, never a secret:

1. **Pair** → host makes a per-pairing X25519 keypair; QR = `{proto:"spd2", …, pid, hpk}`.
2. **Handshake** → phone makes its own ephemeral keypair, ECDHs with `hpk`, HKDF-derives the device key, and `POST /pair {pid, ppk, mac}` where `mac = HMAC(key, pid‖hpk‖ppk)` proves it saw the QR. Host re-derives, checks the tag, and replies **sealed with the key** (authenticating itself back).
3. **Messages** → unchanged AES-256-GCM `/rpc` envelopes with the monotonic counter.

New guarantees on top of what you had:
- **A screenshot of the QR can't derive the key** (needs the phone's ephemeral private key).
- **Forward secrecy** — a later key leak doesn't expose past pairings.
- **No pairing hijack** — an on-path attacker without the QR can't forge the confirmation tag.

## Verified headlessly

| Check | Result |
|---|---|
| `x25519-interop.mjs` (noble ↔ Node agree on shared/HKDF/HMAC) | ✅ |
| Host test-client: bad-confirmation **hijack rejected** | ✅ |
| …plus forgery / tamper / replay rejected, real pairing + action | ✅ |
| `interop-check.mjs` (AES-GCM both ways) | ✅ |
| `tsc --noEmit` (phone) · Electron boots on spd2 | ✅ |

Touched host, phone crypto/rpc, scan handler, the pairing window, test client, and docs. All auto-committed to `main`.

## What's left (unchanged, minus this)

**Yours (hardware/accounts):** the first real on-device run · iOS EAS setup (`eas init` + `EXPO_TOKEN`) · signed installers.
**Optional builds I can take:** Windows keystroke support (`keys` is macOS-only) · `shell` allowlist/confirmation · phone polish (auto-reconnect, reorder tiles, multi-page).

The security story is now solid end-to-end. My honest take: the **device run** is the highest-value next step — it's the only unproven link. Want me to knock out **Windows keystroke support** next (so the host isn't macOS-only), or is there something else you'd rather I take?

```diff
- async function sendKeys(combo) {
  if (platform !== "darwin") {
    throw new Error(`Key shortcuts are only implemented for macOS (got ${platform})`);
  }
  return sh(`osascript -e ${q(comboToAppleScript(combo))}`);
}
+ async function sendKeys(combo) {
  if (platform === "darwin") return sh(`osascript -e ${q(comboToAppleScript(combo))}`);
  if (platform === "win32") return sendKeysWindows(combo);
  throw new Error(`Key shortcuts aren't implemented for ${platform}`);
}

// The SendKeys string is passed via an env var so we never have to escape it
// through the shell — PowerShell reads $env:SPD_KEYS.
async function sendKeysWindows(combo) {
  const script =
    "Add-Type -AssemblyName System.Windows.Forms; [System.Windows.Forms.SendKeys]::SendWait($env:SPD_KEYS)";
  const { stderr } = await execFileAsync("powershell", ["-NoProfile", "-STA", "-Command", script], {
    env: { ...process.env, SPD_KEYS: comboToSendKeys(combo) },
    windowsHide: true,
  });
  if (stderr && stderr.trim()) console.error("  stderr:", stderr.trim());
}
```

```diff
};

// Characters that are special in SendKeys and must be wrapped in braces.
const WIN_ESCAPE = new Set(["+", "^", "%", "~", "(", ")", "{", "}", "[", "]"]);

export function comboToSendKeys(combo) {
  const parts = combo.toLowerCase().split("+").map((p) => p.trim()).filter(Boolean);

    else key = part;
```

### 🤖 Claude · 12:19:16

**Windows keystroke support is done** — `keys` actions now work on Windows too, so the host is no longer macOS-only.

## What changed (`desktop/src/executor.mjs`)

- `sendKeys` dispatches by platform: macOS → AppleScript (unchanged), **Windows → PowerShell `SendKeys`** (built-in, no native deps).
- New `comboToSendKeys()` maps our combo format to SendKeys syntax (`^` Ctrl, `%` Alt, `+` Shift), with special keys (`{ENTER}`, `{F4}`, `{LEFT}`, …) and brace-escaping for literals.
- The SendKeys string is passed via an **env var** (`$env:SPD_KEYS`), so nothing gets mangled through shell quoting.
- ✅ Verified the mapping headlessly — `cmd+space`→`^ `, `cmd+shift+4`→`^+4`, `alt+f4`→`%{F4}`, unknown keys throw, etc. (Actual key injection needs a real Windows box — yours to confirm.)

**Known Windows limits** (documented): "cmd" maps to Ctrl, and the Win/Super key and Ctrl+Alt+Del can't be synthesized. Linux still has no keystroke path.

## Status of what's left

**Yours (hardware/accounts):** the on-device run · iOS EAS setup · signed installers.
**Optional builds remaining:**
- **`shell` safety** — it's arbitrary RCE (gated only by pairing); an allowlist or a per-press confirmation on the host would harden it.
- **Phone polish** — auto-reconnect + retry, drag-to-reorder tiles, multi-page decks, haptics.

Everything's auto-committed to `main`.

I'll keep going if you say so — my pick for the next one is **`shell` safety** (it's the last real security sharp edge). But I want to flag honestly: I've now built well past the original plan, and the **one thing that's never been proven is a real press from a real phone**. If you can do that device run, it's worth more than any more code I write blind. Want me to take **shell safety**, **phone polish**, or pause here for you to run it on a device?

```diff
      } catch (e) {
        handled.current = false;
        setError(e instanceof Error ? e.message : String(e));
      }
    },
    [onPaired],
  );
```

### 🤖 Claude · 10:48:10

Everything's staged for the simulator run. Current state:

- ✅ **Host restarted** with the new **"Copy pairing data"** button — listening on `:8788`.
- ✅ **Manual-pair fallback added** to the app (tsc clean) — it'll hot-reload once Metro is up, since the Simulator has no camera to scan the QR.
- ⏳ **iOS build still compiling** (currently the expo-dev-menu pods). When it finishes it'll auto-install to the iPhone 17 Pro simulator, launch, and start Metro. I'll confirm when it actually appears.

**When the app opens in the simulator, here's the pairing flow:**

1. On the Mac's **Pair a phone** window → click **Copy pairing data** (this copies the JSON; the Mac clipboard syncs into the Simulator).
2. In the app → tap **Enter pairing data manually** → tap the field and **paste** (⌘V) → **Pair**.
3. You should jump to the deck and see **"Connected to MacBookPro"**; the Mac window flips to **Paired ✓**.
4. Tap a tile — the Mac reacts.

Two heads-ups:
- The host is in **real mode**, so tiles actually do things. Start with **Google** (just opens a URL — harmless). Others like **Screenshot** / **Mute** / **Spotlight** really fire. Say the word and I'll relaunch the host in **dry-run** (logs `▶ …` instead of executing) for a safe first test.
- If the app says **"can't reach host"**, the simulator sometimes can't hit the Mac's LAN IP — in the pasted JSON change `"host":"<LAN-IP>"` to `"host":"127.0.0.1"` and pair again.

I'll report back the moment the build lands the app in the simulator.

```diff
- const VALID_TYPES = new Set(["app", "url", "keys", "shell"]);

/** Minimal shape check mirroring the Next app's validateAction intent. */
export function validateAction(action) {
  if (!action || typeof action !== "object") return null;
  const { type } = action;
  if (!VALID_TYPES.has(type)) return null;
  const field = type === "app" || type === "url" ? "target" : type === "keys" ? "combo" : "command";
  const value = action[field];
  if (typeof value !== "string" || value.length === 0 || value.length > 4096) return null;
  return { type, [field]: value };
}
+ const FIELD_BY_TYPE = { app: "target", url: "target", keys: "combo", shell: "command", text: "text" };

/** Minimal shape check mirroring the Next app's validateAction intent. */
export function validateAction(action) {
  if (!action || typeof action !== "object") return null;
  const field = FIELD_BY_TYPE[action.type];
  if (!field) return null;
  const value = action[field];
  if (typeof value !== "string" || value.length === 0 || value.length > 4096) return null;
  return { type: action.type, [field]: value };
}
```

Now I need to (a) restart the host so it loads the new executor, and (b) reload the app so the new JS (Send-text bar + `text` type) is in the bundle. Restarting the host **keeps existing pairings** (device keys persist in `devices.json`). Stopping it and checking pairing state:

**🔧 Bash**

Both `keys` (Spotlight/Screenshot/Lock tiles) and the new `text` action send keystrokes, and macOS gates that on **Accessibility permission**. **🔧 Edit**

```diff
- - **`keys`**: macOS via AppleScript (needs Accessibility permission — grant on the
  first shortcut action); **Windows** via PowerShell `SendKeys`. On Windows "cmd"
  maps to Ctrl, and the Win/Super key + secure sequences (Ctrl+Alt+Del) can't be
  synthesized. Linux has no keystroke path yet.
+ - **`keys` / `text`**: macOS via AppleScript `keystroke` (needs **Accessibility**
  permission — grant the host under System Settings → Privacy & Security →
  Accessibility, or the keystroke silently blocks/prompts); **Windows** via
  PowerShell `SendKeys`. On Windows "cmd" maps to Ctrl, and the Win/Super key +
  secure sequences (Ctrl+Alt+Del) can't be synthesized. Linux has no keystroke
  path yet. `text` types a literal string into the focused app.
```

### 👤 User · 12:56:54

do I need to restart simulator app?

### 🤖 Claude · 12:57:28

Short answer: **no — not the simulator app.** The phone app just sends the text over the network; the pairing and connection persist. Nothing to restart there.

The permission is on the **Mac side** (the process that actually presses the keys), and there are two things worth knowing:

1. **The host may need a restart to pick up the grant.** macOS caches the Accessibility decision per running process, so after you flip the toggle on, the host process should be restarted to see it. I launched it, so **I can restart it for you** once you've granted the permission — just say go.

2. **The dialog you saw was from my test, not the host.** That prompt was triggered by the throwaway `node` test I ran. The **Electron host** is a *different* process, so:
   - In the Accessibility list (System Settings → Privacy & Security → Accessibility), the entry to enable in dev mode usually shows up as **Electron** (or the terminal that launched it), not "streamPhoneDeck".
   - The **first time you tap Send**, the host itself may pop its *own* Accessibility prompt — allow that, then I restart the host.

So the clean sequence:
1. Leave the simulator as-is.
2. Grant Accessibility to **Electron** (or your terminal) in System Settings.
3. Tell me "go" → I restart the Mac host.
4. Click into a text field on the Mac, then tap **Send** in the simulator.

Want me to restart the host now, or wait until you've toggled the permission?

- **Host:** it checks Accessibility **at the moment it types**, not at launch — and since your terminal (which launched the host) already has the permission, the host's keystrokes should go through as-is. The host's `text`/`keys` path only talks to **System Events** (Accessibility), so it won't hit an Automation prompt.
- **That earlier hang was a red herring** — my *test* additionally tried to script TextEdit directly (`tell application "TextEdit" …`), which needs the separate **Automation** consent, and that's what blocked. The real host path doesn't do that.

### 🤖 Claude · 13:08:24

**Done — and it hot-reloaded straight into the simulator** (no restart needed). You can see the new **"Delay [0] sec before send"** row with **3 / 5 / 10** quick-presets right under the Send bar.

I also spotted your **"Text- mango"** tile in the deck — so you've already confirmed the `text` action works as a saved tile too. 👍

## How the wait timer works

- Set a delay — tap a preset (**3/5/10**) or type any number in the box (0–60s).
- Tap **Send**:
  - **0s** → sends immediately (unchanged).
  - **>0s** → starts a countdown. The button turns into **"Cancel 5s… 4s…"** and the status shows *"Sending in Ns… (focus the target on your Mac)"*. When it hits 0, the text is typed on the Mac.
- **Cancel** anytime by tapping the button during the countdown. It also auto-clears if you leave the screen.

It's a **client-side** delay (the phone counts down, then sends), which is exactly what you want: tap Send, switch to the right field/app on your Mac, and it fires when the timer ends.

## Try it
Type something → tap **5** → **Send** → click into a Notes/TextEdit window on the Mac → watch it type after 5 seconds.

tsc is clean and it's committed to `main`. Want me to also add a per-tile delay option (so `text`/`keys` *tiles* can have their own built-in wait), or is the Send-bar timer enough?

### 👤 User · 13:21:57

great! How do I install it in my phone for testing?

### 🤖 Claude · 13:22:54

Now that it's a real phone, two nice things change: you'll **scan the QR with the actual camera** (no more manual paste), and the phone just needs to be on the **same Wi-Fi** as your Mac (plus Accessibility granted on the Mac for text/shortcuts).

There are two "flavors" of build — pick based on how you want to use it:

| | **Tethered dev build** | **Standalone build** |
|---|---|---|
| JS comes from | `expo start` on your Mac (Metro) | bundled into the app |
| Needs computer running? | Yes, while using it | No — just the phone |
| Best for | quick iteration | actually using it day-to-day |

## iOS (you have a Mac + Xcode)

**Fastest (free, tethered):**
```bash
cd mobile
npx expo run:ios --device        # plug in the iPhone, pick it from the list
```
First time, Xcode needs a signing team — open `mobile/ios/*.xcworkspace`, select the target → Signing & Capabilities → pick your **Apple ID (Personal Team)**. Free accounts work but the app expires after **7 days** (just rebuild). Keep `npx expo start --dev-client` running while you use it.

**Standalone iOS** needs an **Apple Developer account** ($99/yr) to register your device: `npx eas device:create` then `eas build -p ios --profile preview`.

## Android (easiest — no account)

**Fastest (tethered):** enable USB debugging, plug in, then:
```bash
cd mobile && npx expo run:android --device
```

**Standalone APK (no computer needed):**
```bash
cd mobile && npx eas login          # free Expo account
npx eas build -p android --profile preview
```
That gives a download link → install the APK on your phone (allow "install unknown apps") → open it, scan the QR, done. This is the one I'd recommend if you want to just **use** it — it runs on its own and only needs your Mac's host app + same Wi-Fi.

---

**My pick:** if you have an Android phone, `eas build -p android --profile preview` is the cleanest standalone. If it's iPhone-only, `npx expo run:ios --device` is the quickest to try today (just keep Metro running).

Which phone do you want to target? Plug it in and I can kick off the right command for you (I'll handle everything except the physical plug-in and, on iOS, the one-time signing-team click).

### 🤖 Claude · 13:26:52

That error is actually a simple, well-known iOS gotcha — the build itself is fine (signing worked with your Apple ID, 0 errors). The device just needs **Developer Mode** turned on. It's right there in the log:

> `SDphone … error: Developer Mode disabled — enable Developer Mode in Settings → Privacy & Security.`

iOS 16+ requires this before it'll run development builds.

## Fix (on the iPhone, ~1 min)

1. **Settings → Privacy & Security →** scroll to the bottom → **Developer Mode** → toggle **On**.
   - *(That menu only appears after a dev build has been attempted — which you just did, so it'll be there now.)*
2. It prompts to **restart** the phone → restart.
3. After it reboots, a prompt asks **"Turn on Developer Mode?"** → tap **Turn On** and enter your passcode.
4. Unlock the phone and keep it plugged in.

Then re-run:
```bash
npx expo run:ios --device
```

## Two more likely speed-bumps (so they don't surprise you)

- **First launch → "Untrusted Developer":** on the phone go to **Settings → General → VPN & Device Management →** tap your Apple ID (dekisuki05@gmail.com) → **Trust**.
- **Keep Metro running:** this is a dev build, so JS loads from your Mac — leave `npx expo start --dev-client` running, and keep the phone on the **same Wi-Fi** as the Mac (also required to reach the deck host).

Once it launches, you'll finally get the real flow: open the Mac's **Pair a phone** window and **scan the QR with the camera** — no manual paste needed.

Flip on Developer Mode and restart the phone; when it's back, say the word and I'll re-run `npx expo run:ios --device` for you.

### 👤 User · 13:50:42

stuck on codesign wants to access key "Apple Development: shubham Arun Dekatey

### 🤖 Claude · 13:50:58

That's just the macOS keychain guarding your signing key — click **"Always Allow"** (not "Allow"), and enter your Mac login password if it asks.

- **Always Allow** = adds `codesign` to that key's access list, so it won't nag you on every future build.
- **Allow** works too, but you'll get this popup again next build.

If it **keeps reappearing** or the dialog seems stuck:
1. Open **Keychain Access** (Spotlight → "Keychain Access").
2. Left sidebar: select the **login** keychain → category **Keys** (or search `Apple Development`).
3. Find the private key **"Apple Development: shubham Arun Dekatey …"** → double-click it → **Access Control** tab.
4. Choose **"Allow all applications to access this item"** (or click **+** and add `/usr/bin/codesign`) → **Save Changes** (enter your Mac password).
5. Re-run `npx expo run:ios --device 00008150-00060D901E3A401C`.

Also make sure your **Mac isn't locked** while it builds — a locked login keychain is a common reason these prompts hang.

Click **Always Allow** and it should continue the build. Paste the next output and I'll keep going.

### 🤖 Claude · 15:40:29

Both failing together points at one thing: **the phone can't reach the Mac host** (manual entry uses the exact same network call as the QR path, so if pairing fails silently for both, it's the connection, not the scanner). On a real phone there are two new gates that the simulator didn't have — **iOS Local Network permission** and the **Mac firewall** — plus the phone must be on the **same Wi-Fi**.

Let me diagnose from the Mac side first:

**🔧 Bash**
*Diagnose host reachability and firewall*
```bash
echo "=== Mac Wi-Fi IP ==="; ipconfig getifaddr en0 2>/dev/null; echo "=== host's advertised IP (pairing.json) ==="; PJ="$HOME/Library/Application Support/streamPhoneDeck/data/pairing.json"; node -e "const p=require(process.argv[1]);console.log('host:',p.host,'port:',p.port,'proto:',p.proto)" "$PJ" 2>/dev/null || echo "(no pairing.json)"; echo "=== is host reachable on its LAN IP? ==="; LANIP=$(node -e "console.log(require(process.argv[1]).host)" "$PJ" 2>/dev/null); curl -m 4 -s "http://$LANIP:8788/health" && echo " <- LAN reachable" || echo "LAN curl FAILED"; echo "=== and on loopback? ==="; curl -m 4 -s "http://127.0.0.1:8788/health" && echo " <- loopback reachable" || echo "loopback FAILED"; echo "=== macOS app firewall ==="; /usr/libexec/ApplicationFirewall/socketfilterfw --getglobalstate 2>/dev/null; /usr/libexec/ApplicationFirewall/socketfilterfw --getblockall 2>/dev/null
```

### 🤖 Claude · 15:41:46

The Mac side is **healthy**: host responds on `192.168.1.153:8788`, and the macOS firewall is **off**. So the host isn't the problem — it's the **iPhone reaching it**. The two real suspects on a physical phone (neither existed in the simulator):

1. **iOS Local Network permission** — the app needs explicit permission to talk to LAN devices; if it's off/denied, every connection to `192.168.1.x` is silently blocked.
2. **Phone not on the same Wi-Fi** (cellular, or a guest/isolated network).

First, let me make the app **tell you** what's wrong instead of hanging silently — adding a timeout + a clear error to the network calls (this hot-reloads to your phone via Metro):

**🔧 Edit**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/mobile/src/rpc.ts`
```diff
- export type DeckAction = ButtonAction;
+ export type DeckAction = ButtonAction;

// POST JSON with a timeout so an unreachable host surfaces a clear message
// instead of hanging. On a real phone the usual cause is the wrong Wi-Fi or the
// iOS "Local Network" permission being off.
async function postJson(url: string, body: unknown, timeoutMs = 8000): Promise<Response> {
  const controller = new AbortController();
  const timer = setTimeout(() => controller.abort(), timeoutMs);
  try {
    return await fetch(url, {
      method: "POST",
      headers: { "content-type": "application/json" },
      body: JSON.stringify(body),
      signal: controller.signal,
    });
  } catch {
    const host = url.replace(/^https?:\/\//, "").split("/")[0];
    throw new Error(`Can't reach ${host}. Same Wi-Fi as the Mac? Local Network permission on?`);
  } finally {
    clearTimeout(timer);
  }
}
```

### 🤖 Claude · 15:42:45

Clean. That timeout/error will hot-reload to your phone, so pairing now fails *fast with a message* instead of hanging.

Here's the situation and how to fix it:

## Diagnosis
Your Mac host is **fine** — it answers on `192.168.1.153:8788` and the firewall is off. The break is the **iPhone → Mac** hop.

## Quickest test — isolate it in 5 seconds
On the **iPhone, open Safari** and go to:
```
http://192.168.1.153:8788/health
```
- **See `{"ok":true,...}`** → the network is fine, so it's the **app's Local Network permission** → do step 2 below.
- **Doesn't load** → it's **Wi-Fi/network** → do step 1 below.

## Fixes (on the iPhone)

**1. Same Wi-Fi.** Settings → Wi-Fi → make sure it's the **same network as the Mac** (the `192.168.1.x` one) — not cellular, not a "Guest" network. (Also make sure the Mac isn't on Ethernet-only / a different SSID.)

**2. Local Network permission.** Settings → scroll to **streamPhoneDeck** → turn **Local Network ON** (and confirm **Camera** ON for the QR). If "Local Network" isn't listed yet, tap **Pair** once — iOS should pop *"…would like to find and connect to devices on your local network"* → tap **Allow**. If you'd tapped "Don't Allow" earlier, the Settings toggle is how you undo it — then fully close and reopen the app.

## Also
- The QR/code has a **~2-min TTL** — if the Mac's pairing window shows a countdown that's expired, click **New code** before scanning again.
- With the new build, when you tap **Pair** you'll now get a clear message within ~8s (e.g., *"Can't reach 192.168.1.153:8788…"*) — tell me exactly what it says and I'll pinpoint it.

Do the Safari test first and tell me which way it goes — that instantly tells us whether it's the network or the app permission.

### 👤 User · 15:51:23

how can someone install an app on macos?

### 🤖 Claude · 15:52:10

Good question — and it matters for your streamPhoneDeck **desktop host**, which you'd want people to install rather than run via `npm start`. Here are the ways macOS apps get installed, then how it applies to yours.

## The common methods

| Method | How it works | Typical for |
|---|---|---|
| **.dmg (drag-install)** | Open the disk image, drag the `.app` into **Applications** | Most indie/dev apps (what electron-builder makes) |
| **.pkg installer** | Double-click → guided installer runs scripts | Apps that install system components/CLIs |
| **Mac App Store** | Install button in the Store | Consumer apps (needs Apple review + notarization) |
| **Homebrew Cask** | `brew install --cask <name>` | Developer-distributed tools |
| **Bare `.app`** | Just drag a `.app` bundle into Applications | Simple apps |

## For streamPhoneDeck specifically

You already have the packaging set up. Build the installer:
```bash
cd desktop && npm run dist:mac
```
That produces `desktop/dist/streamPhoneDeck-0.1.0.dmg`. A user then: **open the .dmg → drag `streamPhoneDeck.app` to Applications → launch it.**

## The catch: it's **unsigned**, so Gatekeeper blocks it

Your build uses `identity: null` (no Apple signing), so on another Mac the first launch shows *"Apple could not verify streamPhoneDeck is free of malware"* (or *"is damaged"*). Two levels of fix:

- **Quick, per-user bypass:** launch it, then **System Settings → Privacy & Security → "Open Anyway"** (on macOS 15+, the old right-click→Open trick often won't cut it). If it says *"damaged"*, that's the quarantine flag: `xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine /Applications/streamPhoneDeck.app`.
- **Proper distribution (opens cleanly for everyone):** sign with an **Apple Developer ID** cert + **notarize**. With electron-builder that's setting `mac.identity` to your Developer ID and adding notarization creds — needs the paid **Apple Developer account** ($99/yr), same one that'd unblock your iOS device builds.

---

Want me to:
1. **Build the `.dmg` now** so you have an installer to test?
2. Or set up **Developer ID signing + notarization** in `electron-builder` (needs your paid Apple account) so it installs without warnings?

(For just *you* testing on this Mac, running `cd desktop && npm start` — or `npm run pack` for an unpacked `.app` — is still the least friction.)

```
name: desktop-build

# Builds the installable desktop host with electron-builder on native runners and
# uploads the installers as artifacts you can download from the run's summary.
#   macOS   → .dmg   (Apple Silicon; unsigned — see note below)
#   Windows → .exe   (NSIS installer; unsigned)
#
# Builds are UNSIGNED (CSC_IDENTITY_AUTO_DISCOVERY=false). On first launch users
# get a Gatekeeper/SmartScreen warning; for clean distribution add a Developer ID
# (macOS) / code-signing cert (Windows) via repo secrets and electron-builder's
# signing config. Artifacts expire after 90 days — tag a release to keep them.

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths:
      - "desktop/**"
      - ".github/workflows/desktop-build.yml"

jobs:
  build:
    name: ${{ matrix.os }}
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false
      matrix:
        include:
          - os: macos-latest
            artifact: streamphonedeck-macos-dmg
            path: desktop/dist/*.dmg
          - os: windows-latest
            artifact: streamphonedeck-windows-installer
            path: desktop/dist/*.exe
    runs-on: ${{ matrix.os }}
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: desktop
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 20
          cache: npm
          cache-dependency-path: desktop/package-lock.json

      - name: Install dependencies
        run: npm ci

      - name: Build installer (unsigned)
        e

```

````
# streamPhoneDeck

Turn your phone into a [Stream Deck](https://www.elgato.com/stream-deck)-style
control surface. Tap a button on your phone and it **launches an app, opens a URL,
sends a keyboard shortcut, or types text on your computer** — in real time over
your local network, on an **end-to-end encrypted** connection you set up by
**scanning a QR code**.

## The two apps

| Part | What it is | Tech | Folder |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **Phone app** | Native iOS/Android deck — scans a QR to pair, then sends button presses | Expo / React Native (TS) | [`mobile/`](mobile/) |
| **Desktop host** | Installable menu-bar/tray app that pairs with the phone and runs the actions | Electron + electron-builder | [`desktop/`](desktop/) |

```
native phone app  ──AES-256-GCM over HTTP (per-device key)──▶  desktop host  ──▶  macOS / Windows
   scan QR to pair          monotonic counter, X25519 handshake        tray + pairing window
```

> There's also a **legacy browser-based deck** (Next.js) in `src/` + `host-agent/`
> — the original version. It still works but is superseded by the native app +
> desktop host below. See [Legacy web deck](#legacy-web-deck).

## Action types

| Type | Example | What it does | Platforms |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `app` | `{ type: "app", target: "Safari" }` | Launch/focus an app | macOS · Windows · Linux |
| `url` | `{ type: "url", target: "https://github.com" }` | Open a URL | all |
| `keys` | `{ type: "keys", combo: "cmd+shift+4" }` | Send a keyboard
… [truncated, 4511 more chars]
````

**🔧 Write**
`/Users/shubhamarundekatey/ClaudeCode/day27/streamPhoneDeck/desktop/build/notarize.cjs`
```
// electron-builder afterSign hook — notarizes the macOS .app with Apple's
// notarytool, but ONLY when the Apple credentials are present in the environment.
// That keeps unsigned builds (no secrets configured) working: the hook just
// skips. Requires the app to be Developer ID-signed first (electron-builder does
// that when CSC_LINK/CSC_KEY_PASSWORD are set).

const { notarize } = require("@electron/notarize");

exports.default = async function notarizing(context) {
  if (context.electronPlatformName !== "darwin") return;

  const { APPLE_ID, APPLE_APP_SPECIFIC_PASSWORD, APPLE_TEAM_ID } = process.env;
  if (!APPLE_ID || !APPLE_APP_SPECIFIC_PASSWORD || !APPLE_TEAM_ID) {
    console.log("  • notarize: skipped (APPLE_ID / APPLE_APP_SPECIFIC_PASSWORD / APPLE_TEAM_ID not set)");
    return;
  }

  const appName = context.packager.appInfo.productFilename;
  console.log(`  • notarizing ${appName}.app (this can take a few minutes)…`);
  await notarize({
    tool: "notarytool",
    appPath: `${context.appOutDir}/${appName}.app`,
    appleId: APPLE_ID,
    appleIdPassword: APPLE_APP_SPECIFIC_PASSWORD,
    teamId: APPLE_TEAM_ID,
  });
  console.log("  • notarization complete");
};
```

```

```
